Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: ICE Brent highest since April on Middle East supply risks
ICE December Brent tops $116/b Hormuz transits climb to 23 on Oct. 22
Prompt-dated ICE Brent settled at the highest level since late April Oct. 23, as geopolitical risk premiums mounted amid concerns that escalating Middle East tensions could impact supply.
ICE December Brent climbed $3.55/b to settle at $116.72/b, the highest level since April 29, while NYMEX December WTI ended the session up $3.80/b at $103.27/b.
"We continue to ascribe rising crude oil prices to inventory depletion and escalation risk, and we retain our upside bias," Clearview Energy Partners analysts said in an Oct. 23 note. "The prospect of kinetic engagement deferred until after the election has loomed since August, but traders may be pricing it in more aggressively as polling in Israel and the US grows closer."
Middle Eastern oil exports have recovered to nearly 75% of prewar levels during the first half of October, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, but a regional escalation would likely impact these flows.
"Tankers have been attacked, there have been reports of mines near the Strait of Hormuz, Saudi facilities have been hit, embassy personnel have been moved and the market is still digesting the possibility of renewed US-Israeli strikes on Iran after the midterms," Quintex global strategist Stephen Innes said in an Oct. 23 note.
NYMEX November RBOB settled 3.51 cents/gallon higher at $3.2116/gal and November ULSD climbed 5.21 cents/gal to $4.9842/gal.

International Crude
FACTBOX: Houthi attacks on Saudi oil, gas assets fuel price risk premium
Brent futures top $116/b as Houthis claim fresh strikes
Yanbu, East-West Pipeline critical amid Hormuz disruption
Saudi crude rerouting north via Suez as Red Sea risks climb
Oil prices have climbed as renewed Houthi attacks raise risks to Saudi Arabia's oil infrastructure and the export routes the kingdom has increasingly relied on amid disruption to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz.
Saudi crude continues to reach international markets, including via Yanbu, the Suez Canal and Egypt's SUMED pipeline, but the latest attacks have put the market on edge over both production infrastructure and alternative export routes out of the Middle East.
"The threat is no longer one route or one front: Iran, the Houthis in Yemen and militias in Iraq can all put Middle East oil infrastructure at risk," according to the latest crude oil market outlook from S&P Global CERA.
The following are key facts about the attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure and their market impact:
Infrastructure Repeated Houthi strikes have targeted Saudi Arabia's core
production and export facilities, raising fears of disruption to crude flows.
Yemen-based Houthis on Oct. 22 claimed an attack on Saudi Aramco facilities in Yanbu and said a fire broke out at the targeted site. Saudi authorities and Aramco have not confirmed an attack or damage as of Oct. 23. Aramco did not respond to a request for comment Oct. 22.
Yanbu is home to Aramco's 400,000 barrels/day Samref refinery, its 400,000 b/d Yasref refinery and the 228,000 b/d Yanbu refinery, as well as crude export infrastructure.
Saudi Arabia's East-West Pipeline, which is critical for rerouting crude exports toward Saudi Arabia's Red Sea port of Yanbu by bypassing the Strait of Hormuz, was shut following Sept. 10 drone attacks. In early October, flow rates through the 7 million b/d capacity pipeline had climbed to 5.8 million b/d after repairs had been carried out, Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman said.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

The SUMED Pipeline in Egypt carries up to 2.5 million b/d northbound from the Red Sea to the Mediterranean, supporting Saudi crude flows toward European and other markets.
On Oct. 19, the Houthis said they had targeted Aramco facilities in Yanbu and the Shaybah oil field with "ballistic and cruise missiles and drones," adding that fires had erupted at the sites. A few days prior, Houthis attacked Aramco sites in Dhahran, home to the company's headquarters, and in Riyadh, as well as an Aramco administrative building.
Trade flows Shipping attacks have forced sharp declines in regional
exports, prompting Aramco to reroute barrels and reassure Asian buyers. With the recent rise in attacks on shipping, Middle East crude
exports have averaged 11.9 million b/d so far in October, down from 15 million b/d in September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Aramco has committed at least 24 million barrels of crude for ship-to-ship transfers off Sohar, Oman, to Asian customers in September and October. The increase in traffic through Suez "closely coincides with escalating Houthi attacks targeting Saudi shipping near the Bab al-Mandab strait," CAS said in a Oct. 20 report. The Houthis declared a naval blockade on Saudi shipping on July 20.

Aramco had shifted crude flows to Yanbu on the Red Sea after shipping disruptions through the Strait of Hormuz curtailed exports from its east coast terminals.
Saudi crude shipments moving north through the Suez Canal reached about 653,000 b/d over Oct. 1-19, according to CAS data, accounting for the majority of northbound crude flows.
Saudi Arabia's visible gross crude loadings have rebounded sharply in October, as Yanbu exports recovered following a pipeline attack that disrupted flows in September, according to CAS data Oct. 19.
In its Oct. 14 monthly report, the International Energy Agency said Saudi Arabian crude supply in September had surged 2.3 million b/d month over month to 8.3 million b/d, the highest level since February, as production recovered from multiple attacks on oil infrastructure.
Prices Crude benchmarks have jumped on fears that further attacks
could disrupt Saudi supply, with Platts assessments capturing the premium buyers are paying.
ICE December Brent crude futures rose above $116/b at 14:25 GMT Oct. 23, up 2.5% from the previous close, while the NYMEX December light sweet crude contract was up 2.7% at $102.19/b.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $122.44/b on Oct. 22, up 2.21% day over day.
Aramco set the Arab Light official selling price to Asia for September at $111.89/b, up 25% from the August OSP.
The VLCC rate for shipping 270,000 metric tons of crude from Yanbu to the Far East was assessed at $198.63/mt Oct. 22, down 16.7% month over month but up sharply from $36.42/mt on July 20, according to Platts data.
Platts assessed the Sidi Kerir-Far East VLCC rate at $230.77/ mt on Oct. 22, up 24.6% from $185.19/mt on Sept. 25 when the assessment began.
The LR1 rate for transporting 55,000 mt of clean petroleum products from the Red Sea to Japan was assessed at $250.80/mt on Oct. 22, up from $186.85/mt on Sept. 22 and $50.16/mt on July 20.
-- Lauren Holtmeier
CPC Blend discount to Urals falls to 6-year low as sweet differentials correct
Urals prices stabilize amid balanced sour complex
CPC Blend differentials fall on weak demand
The discount paid for Kazakh CPC Blend FOB Novorossiysk over Russian Urals fell to its lowest level in over six years on Oct. 23, as the Mediterranean sour complex remains balanced while sweet crude differentials experience a sharp correction.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

3

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Urals FOB Novorossiysk at $25.35/barrel discount to Dated Brent Oct. 23, over $6/b above CPC Blend FOB Novorossiysk, which was at minus $31.90/b as high freight, limited demand and increased MOC offering depressed the differentials for the flagship Kazakh grade. The spread is the lowest since April 28, 2020, when Platts assessed it at minus $7.585/b.
The spread between sour Urals and sweet CPC has been narrowing since late September but has flipped only in recent days as CPC's prices for the November trading cycle corrected.
This comes as the sour crude complex in the Mediterranean and Black Sea remains balanced amid stable flows from the Middle East and improvements in flows from Saudi Arabia.
"There is oil, I don't see scarcity at the moment," a Mediterranean trader said.

With the market past the disruption caused in September by the attack on the East-West pipeline, sour crude differentials have stabilized with Basrah Medium heard offered flat to November average Dated Brent for the past week and Kirkuk valued at a small premium, according to heard values published by Platts.
Market participants also commented on the improved availability of Saudi crude as exports rebound, but these additional flows are yet to push sour differentials significantly lower as most of the correction in Mediterranean sours took place in late September and early October.
Urals price DAP India has stabilized throughout October at around a $3/b premium to the calendar month average Dated Brent, as improved sour supply from the Middle East has forced Russian crude to price more competitively.
Meanwhile, the Mediterranean sweets corrected heavily over the last week, pushing Azeri Light and then CPC Blend a few dollars lower in a matter of days as traders struggled to clear cargoes loading in early November.
CPC Blend CIF Augusta differential, which averaged $0.93/b in September, fell from $1.76/b Oct. 15 to minus $6.855/b Oct. 22, Platts data showed. For the delivered Suezmax cargoes, the price reached its lowest level since the assessment was launched on June 2, 2025.
The correction in CPC prices is part of a broader trend in the Mediterranean sweet market, but the fall in delivered prices has been particularly sharp.
"The market seems pretty depressed at the moment, but sometimes it is just sentiment, in addition to freight and structure, of course," a second Med trader said.

With differentials declining rapidly, market participants found it hard to make predictions about future prices, as they focused on day-to-day moves.
"It's like trying to catch a falling knife," a third Med trader said. -- Jan Kedzior
CPC Blend FOB differentials sink to all-time lows amid pricey freight, ample supply
CIF Augusta price at lowest in nearly four years
Black Sea-Med freight firmly above w1,000
CPC searching for levels that open arbitrages
The FOB differential of Aframax CPC Blend crude oil has fallen to its lowest level on record as high freight costs and rising insurance premiums depress differentials for the flagship Kazakh grade.
Platts assessed CPC Blend FOB Aframax differential at a $28.87/b discount to Dated Brent on Oct. 22, the lowest since the assessment was launched on Nov. 1, 2011, while the price for CPC Blend delivered CIF Augusta fell to minus $6.855/b, the lowest since Dec. 20, 2022.
The FOB price, calculated as a netback from the CIF price using Black Sea-Med freight, Additional War Risk Premium and cost of delays in the Turkish Straits, has been depressed in recent weeks as the market searched for levels which would allow some of the CPC barrels to leave the region.
The loading program, which was reported at around 1.7 million b/d for October and is expected to be broadly similar in November, is too large to be absorbed by the Mediterranean market and relies on exports to Northwest Europe and Asia to clear monthly volumes.
While elevated freight costs have impacted across the crude complex, the situation for Black Sea barrels is particularly acute with Black Sea-Med freight firmly above 1,000 Worldscale points since Oct. 9, compared with around w800 for crossMediterranean routes.
Added to that is the Additional War Risk Premium charged to charterers operating in high-risk areas, which has recently spiked in the Black Sea to around $5/b, up from an average of $2.87/b in September.
"The freight is too high, it's nonsense," one Black Sea trader said.
But despite high transport costs, the market remains very well supplied, and in the absence of particularly strong refining demand, sellers need to compete on price to clear their volumes.
"It is not just sentiment, it is fundamentals - there is a lot of oil," one Mediterranean source said.
Activity in the Platts Market on Close assessment process has also picked up in recent days, driving prices lower every day.
On Oct. 19, Vitol and Chevron offered cargoes delivered Augusta loading Nov. 8-12 and reached a 15 cents/b premium to Dated Brent. The following day the offers were outstanding at minus $2.35/b, then on Oct. 22 BP lifted Vitol's offer at minus

$7/b while Chevron's indication was outstanding at London close at minus $7.10/b.
"There is too much CPC in the Mediterranean," a second Med source said. "It must keep getting lower until it reaches a level at which it can compete in Northwest Europe."
-- Jan Kedzior
Iraq crude exports hold steady in September; UAE emerges as major buyer
India tops China as largest Iraqi oil importer
UAE imports rise to 619,000 b/d in September
Basrah Medium exports reach 1.7 million b/d
Iraq's September crude exports of 2.48 million barrels/day were largely steady month over month, at roughly 75% of prewar levels, according to port loading data obtained exclusively by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The ongoing crisis in the Strait of Hormuz has created new trade flows, with the UAE emerging as one of the biggest buyers of Iraqi crude in September, importing 619,000 b/d of mostly Basrah Medium. The UAE had, until recently, imported only a rare cargo of Iraqi crude to process at its Ruwais refinery. Since June, UAE imports of Iraqi crude have been rising.
China, typically the largest taker of Iraqi crude, was replaced by India, which imported 700,000 b/d, according to the data. China imported 99,000 b/d, importing both of Iraq's key export grades.
Exports of Basrah Medium in September reached 1.7 million b/d, with Basrah Heavy exports totaling 775,800 b/d. Exports of Basrah Heavy climbed 235,000 b/d from 540,000 b/d in August.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Faleh Al-Khayat
CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex sees two convergences
51 Dubai partials trade during MOC
Dec-loading Oman, Murban crude cargoes declared
Medium sour differentials snap 3-day rise
The Middle East sour crude complex saw two convergences during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 23, while cash differentials for key sour crude markers edged lower.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai, Oman and Murban at premiums of $7.99/b to same-month Dubai futures at market close, down 10 cents/b day over day.
During the MOC, 51 December Dubai partials, each comprising 25,000 barrels, were traded. The sellers were Glencore, Unipec, BP, Mitsui and Mercuria, while the buyers were Trafigura, Phillips 66, BP and ExxonMobil.
Unipec declared a cargo of December Oman crude and Glencore declared a cargo of December Murban crude to Trafigura following convergences of 20 partials in Platts Cash Dubai.

US waiver eases risks for India, China, but won't alter Russian crude flows
Waiver to reduce uncertainty over Russian crude: CERA
India, China to maintain Russian crude purchases
Refiners to seek alternatives amid policy risks
The US waiver on Russian crude has reduced uncertainty for Indian and Chinese refiners, giving them greater confidence to continue purchasing economically attractive Russian grades while keeping alternative supply options open in case the situation changes, analysts and industry sources said Oct. 22.
The waiver is expected to preserve, rather than materially alter, Russian crude flows to India and China. While it reduces the possibility of a sudden disruption to global oil supplies, the potential for the waiver to be reversed means political and compliance risks would continue to influence trade, shipping and pricing decisions.
"For refiners in India and China, the waiver is really about reducing the uncertainty rather than changing the crude supply picture," said Premasish Das, executive director for oil analytics at S&P Global Energy CERA. "Russian barrels were already flowing into these markets, but the waiver removes an immediate concern and gives refiners more confidence to continue buying grades that make economic sense."
"However, as the legislation remains in place, refiners may still want to keep alternative supply options open in case the situation changes," Das said.
US President Donald Trump signed an executive order Oct. 16, waiving measures included in the Lindsey O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act for the largest buyers of Russian oil, citing "national interests," days before they were due to come into effect.
The legislation targeted buyers of Russian hydrocarbons, allowing the president to impose tariffs of up to 100% on goods imported from the five largest buyers of Russian crude oil or natural gas by total volume over the year before enactment, among other measures.
Countries such as India and China can continue to import Russian crude without immediately facing the threat of additional

US penalties or tariffs. However, the waiver does not permanently revoke the law, and it can be withdrawn. As a result, buyers may choose to diversify their oil supplies and remain cautious about future sanctions risks, industry sources said.
"Markets are likely to view the waiver as a tactical reprieve rather than a durable policy settlement," said B. Anand, former CEO of Nayara Energy and founder of consultancy firm Meridian Partners. "Past US decisions not to renew waivers reinforce caution: buyers may use the procurement window, but longerterm commitments will depend on greater confidence in policy continuity."
Russian flows to Asia intact Industry sources said India's demand for Russian crude is
expected to remain strong in the near term. However, refiners would continue to diversify their supply sources as insurance against potential future US actions.
"As of now, it is a sixty-day pause only. So, we have to wait and watch how the tri-lateral deliberations of US, Ukraine and Russia unfold. We can hope for the best," said DLN Sastri, former director of oil refining and marketing at the Federation of Indian Petroleum Industry.
Russian crude discharges to India rose sharply in the first half of 2026, increasing from 1.17 million barrels/day in January to a peak of 2.43 million b/d in June, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. Flows eased to 2.05 million b/d in July, 1.98 million b/d in August and 1.64 million b/d in September but remained well above January levels.
Russian crude discharges in China -- including pipeline imports -- remained strong but volatile in 2026, according to General Administration of Customs data.
Flows peaked at 2.72 million b/d in February before declining to 2.38 million b/d in March and 1.93 million b/d in May, GAC data showed. Discharges recovered, reaching 2.65 million b/d in August before moderating to 2.43 million b/d in September, according to GAC data. Despite fluctuations, China's Russian crude intake averaged about 2.35 million b/d over JanuarySeptember, GAC data showed, underscoring sustained demand for discounted Russian barrels.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

China is expected to continue importing Russian crude even without the exemption under the Lindsey O. Graham Act, unless Beijing decides to restrict access, according to Chinese industry sources and analysts.
Tariffs on China's Russian oil purchases could reignite trade tensions with the US while doing little to deter such purchases, given the economic appeal of discounted Russian crude for Chinese refiners.
Russian supplies accounted for 30.9% of China's total crude imports in the first nine months of 2026, GAC data showed.
Additional pressure on supplies A Beijing-based policy analyst specializing in China-Russia
relations said the tariffs may prove ineffective and could drive oil prices higher, putting additional pressure on the global economy.
"It would be another round of the tariff war without the exemption, repeating what happened last year," the analyst said.
The price of ESPO cargoes loaded in the Far East fell to ICE Brent plus $20/b on Oct. 23, down from a premium of $30/b a month ago on a DES Shandong basis, due to improving crude supplies in the global market, according to market sources.
China's independent refiners are expected to continue taking advantage of deeply discounted Russian crude that some Indian buyers and Chinese state-owned companies may avoid, even if the US tariff takes effect, industry sources said.
"We do not export anything to the US, and who does not like cheap feedstocks?" said an independent refiner in Dongying, illustrating why China's smaller refiners are relatively insulated from US trade measures.
China's independent refiners have limited financial exposure to international markets and are barred from exporting refined products, making them less vulnerable to US trade measures than globally active state-owned oil companies.
-- Sambit Mohanty, Oceana Zhou
Petronas sets November Malaysian crude OSP at Dated Brent plus $19.50/b
November premium rises $4.45/b MOM
Tapis differential to MCO OSP at $7.96/b discount
State-owned Petronas has set the November Malaysian crude oil official selling price at a premium of $19.50/barrel to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, up $4.45/b from October, the company said in an Oct. 23 notice.
Petronas also adjusted the price differentials for its secondary crude grades relative to its main crude basket.
The Miri Light differential to the MCO OSP was set at a discount of $4.04/b for November-loading cargoes, compared with a discount of $3.71/b for October, according to the notice.
The company began publishing the price differential for Miri Light crude against the MCO OSP for cargoes loading from October 2025 onward, with the grade remaining in the basket that forms the MCO OSP.

The MCO OSP basket comprises flagship grades, including Labuan, Miri Light, Kikeh and Kimanis crudes.
The Tapis differential to the MCO OSP was set at a discount of $7.96/b for November-loading cargoes, compared with a discount of $7.22/b for October, according to the notice.
The Dulang and Cendor grades were set at premiums of $2.74/b and $2.98/b, respectively, to the MCO OSP for Novemberloading cargoes, compared with premiums of $2.93/b and $2.68/b to the MCO OSP for October.
The Malaysian crude oil OSP differential takes into account a number of factors, such as the average premium or discount to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments in physical spot sales of Labuan, Miri Light, Kikeh and Kimanis grades loading during the month.
Petronas also considers assessments of those grades by price information providers over a specific period, as well as buyers' views on trades and any recommendations they make regarding the value of the crude grades.

SINGAPORE DATA: Crude oil imports rise 69% week over week to 1.14 mil mt
Middle East accounts for 74% of crude oil imports
First Libyan condensate since at least start of 2025
Singapore's crude oil imports rose 69.4% week over week to a nearly three-month high of 1.14 million metric tons over Oct. 15-21, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 22, with the city-state importing its first Libyan condensate since at least the start of 2025.
The Middle East accounted for 74% of total crude oil imports, with 134,666 mt from Saudi Arabia and 714,529 mt from the UAE, according to the data.
Imports were also recorded at 216,997 mt from Brazil and 75,244 mt from Brunei, the data showed.
Total condensate imports for the week were 210,000 mt, with 150,082 mt from Australia and 59,918 mt from Libya, according to the data. This marked the first Libyan condensate shipment to Singapore since at least the start of 2025, Enterprise Singapore data compiled by Platts showed.

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Petronas sets Nov Malaysian crude OSP
Africa lays oil hopes on more tentative exploration pacts
The Asia-Pacific regional complex saw the emergence of Malaysia's official crude price differentials as the trading week inched to a close Oct. 23.
State-owned Petronas has set the November Malaysian crude oil official selling price at a premium of $19.50/barrel to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, up $4.45/b from October, the company said in an Oct. 23 notice.
The Miri Light differential to the MCO OSP was set at a discount of $4.04/b for November-loading cargoes, compared with a discount of $3.71/b for October, according to the notice.
The Tapis differential to the MCO OSP was set at a discount of

$7.96/b for November-loading cargoes, compared with a discount of $7.22/b for October, according to the notice.
The Dulang and Cendor grades were set at premiums of $2.74/b and $2.98/b, respectively, to the MCO OSP for Novemberloading cargoes, compared with premiums of $2.93/b and $2.68/b to the MCO OSP for October.
Meanwhile, with shifting projections around peak oil and wars in Ukraine and Iran spurring renewed interest in African oil and gas exploration, countries across the continent have been working hard to lure international oil companies to their basins in the past year.
Those efforts were on show again at Africa Energy Week -- a giant annual oil and gas conference in Cape Town over Oct. 12-16 -- with countries from Senegal and Algeria to Namibia and Uganda pitching investors on their upstream opportunities.
Against this backdrop, a new deal type has emerged on the continent: low-commitment Memorandums of Understanding to examine geological data across offshore licenses. And the world's largest oil companies have been using these arrangements to hoover up acreage up and down the continent.
Elsewhere, the US waiver on Russian crude has reduced uncertainty for Indian and Chinese refiners, giving them greater confidence to continue purchasing economically attractive Russian grades while keeping alternative supply options open in case the situation changes, analysts and industry sources said Oct. 22.
The waiver is expected to preserve, rather than materially alter, Russian crude flows to India and China. While it reduces the possibility of a sudden disruption to global oil supplies, the potential for the waiver to be reversed means political and compliance risks would continue to influence trade, shipping and pricing decisions.
"For refiners in India and China, the waiver is really about reducing the uncertainty rather than changing the crude supply picture," said Premasish Das, executive director for oil analytics at S&P Global Energy CERA. "Russian barrels were already flowing into these markets, but the waiver removes an immediate concern and gives refiners more confidence to continue buying grades that make economic sense."
-- Leon Wong

Oil NWE and Med Weekly Sour Crude Market Analysis
NWE sours under pressure from ample supply
Middle East flows depress Urals DAP India
The European sour complex remained on a bearish trajectory over the week to Oct. 23 as ample supply limited any potential upside to prices.
Improving inflows of Saudi barrels are expected to keep a lid on any potential upside in prices in Europe, compounding an already oversupplied outlook in the segment, traders said.
This was demonstrated through the Platts Market on Close assessment process with BP offering a first-decade November loading cargo over several sessions, but struggled to attract a buyer.
"I'm guessing a lot of Saudi crude is heading to NWE," said one sour crude trader Oct. 23, noting that the barrels are filling key shorts in the region.

"Either way, there should be somewhat of a correction on JS," the trader continued.
Sour barrels in the Mediterranean, however, struggled to rebound from their earlier decline, but appeared to have found a floor.
Basrah Medium cargoes arriving in November were consistently heard offered flat to November average Dated Brent, while Kirkuk was valued slightly higher.
Differentials could face a further downside risk amid increased offering from Saudi Aramco for flows through the Suez Canal, according to market participants.
Tanker traffic through the Suez Canal has risen to the highest in nearly three years on increased Saudi crude flows, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
Crossings seen in recent weeks were the highest since December 2023, when weekly transits started to collapse from a normal level of 150-160 as Houthi militants launched intense attacks on merchant ships in the Red Sea as part of the IsraelHamas war, Platts reported previously.
Aramco barrels are priced against ICE Brent futures, making them more competitive against crude priced at a differential to Dated Brent. "At the moment, this is the barrel to beat," one Mediterranean source said.
With the punitive freight environment limiting the arbitrage economics of moving Latin American barrels toward Asia, those volumes are pointing toward Europe at competitive levels, traders in both the North Sea and Med have said.
Improved flows from the Middle East have also weighed on Urals, which weakened over the week amid increased competition.
The price for Urals delivered DAP India in December was last heard at around flat to Dated Brent, around $3/b lower week over week.
"Persian Gulf volume is definitely pressuring the prompt market, particularly for India and China," an India-based source said.
While spot prices are lower, traders refrained from making any long-term plans as any escalation in the Middle East could redirect crude flows again.
"It's very difficult to plan anything. When we need to buy, we look at the situation in the market, and we act immediately," the India-based source added.
-- Jan Kedzior, Ernest Puey

Americas Crude
US DOE approves 400,000-barrel SPR exchange for Phillips 66 Lake Charles refinery
Phillips 66 to repay crude plus premium volume
SPR stocks drop to 282.7 million barrels
The US Department of Energy authorized an emergency exchange of up to 400,000 barrels of crude from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve to address a supply disruption affecting Phillips 66's Lake Charles refinery in Louisiana, the department announced Oct. 23.
The crude will be available for immediate delivery from the SPR's West Hackberry site to offset temporary disruptions to refinery crude deliveries and support transportation fuel production for Louisiana and surrounding markets, DOE said in a release.

Phillips 66 will return the borrowed crude along with additional premium barrels.
DOE did not identify the cause or expected duration of the disruption, or specify a repayment schedule or premium volume in its announcement. Spokespeople for DOE and Phillips 66 declined to discuss the specific cause of the refinery disruption.
"Phillips 66 informed DOE that disruptions affecting deliveries through its primary crude oil supply pipeline created an immediate supply challenge for its Lake Charles Refinery," DOE spokesperson Emily Matthews wrote in an email. "The company requested SPR crude to help maintain refinery operations while addressing those supply disruptions."
"We typically do not comment on refinery operations or commercial activity," Phillips 66 spokesperson Al Ortiz wrote in an email. "However, we can confirm we will receive crude oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve. We do not anticipate impacts to Lake Charles operations."
The targeted refinery exchange follows a series of larger SPR solicitations in 2026 under the US commitment to release 172 million barrels as part of a coordinated 400 million-barrel International Energy Agency emergency release.
On Sept. 29, DOE offered up to 40 million barrels from the Big Hill and Bryan Mound sites for delivery in November and December. At the time, the department said five previous solicitations had collectively awarded more than 133 million barrels across four completed exchanges, with earlier exchanges securing a 25% premium in returned barrels. SPR stocks stood at 282.7 million barrels in the week ended Oct. 16, down from 282.9 million barrels a week earlier, according to US Energy Information Administration data.
-- Eamonn Brennan
Argentina's Neuquén sees oil output surge to record high in September
Production surges 26% to 714,089 b/d
Vaca Muerta leads at 97.7% of the total
Gas output rises 9.5% year over year
In Neuquén, Argentina, crude production surged to a record high of 714,089 barrels/day in September from the year-earlier month, led by the Vaca Muerta shale play, while gas rose 9.5% to 104.76 million cubic meters/day over the same period, the province's Energy Ministry said Oct. 23.
Oil production in Neuquén, the country's biggest oil and natural gas province, increased 26% from 566,967 b/d in September 2025, and was up 4.2% from 685,316 b/d in August this year, the ministry said in a statement.
The growth was led by rising output in Vaca Muerta, which produced 697,595 b/d in September, or 97.7% of the province's total crude that month, the ministry said.
The southwestern province's total crude production rose 30% in the first nine months of this year from the same period in 2025, it added.

The month-over-month growth in September was led by increased output in the YPF's Bandurria Sur, Pampa Energía's Rincón de Aranda, YPF's La Angostura Sur I, Vista Energy's Bajada del Palo Oeste, Chevron's El Trapial Este and GeoPark's Loma Jarillosa Este, the ministry said.
Vaca Muerta is driving Argentina's total oil production growth, which rose 15.5% year over year to a record high of 973,700 b/d in September, according to data from the Argentinian Energy Secretariat.
Gas production rises Argentina produced 143.1 MMcm/d of gas in September,
according to the latest data from the national Energy Secretariat. Neuquén's gas production rose 9.5% to 104.76 MMcm/d in
September from 95.71 MMcm/d in the year-earlier month, taking the cumulative year-over-year rise in the first nine months of 2026 to 6.1%.
Of the gas produced in September, Vaca Muerta accounted for 85.97 MMcm/d, or 82.1% of the total, while another 9.75 MMcm/d came from tight gas fields and the rest from conventional reserves, the ministry said.
However, September's gas production was down 9.3% from 115.47 MMcm/d in August, according to ministry records.
The biggest month-over-month production drops in September came from Tecpetrol's Fortín de Piedra, Pampa Energía's Sierra Chata and Pluspetrol's La Calera, the ministry said, adding that the month-over-month decline stems largely from seasonal factors, as rising temperatures reduced residential demand for heating.
Vaca Muerta, in northern Patagonia, is expected to lead a rise in Argentina's oil output to 1.7 million b/d in 2030, while gas is expected to increase to 281 MMcm/d, according to a forecast from the Argentinian Chamber of Hydrocarbons Exploration and Production, an industry group.

Gasoline
European gasoline forward cracks converge amid diminishing blend value
Gasoline cracks compress across forward curve
Gasoline-naphtha spreads converge as blending incentive eases
European gasoline cracks converged sharply across the prompt forward curve over the week ended Oct. 23, while elevated freight costs have made it increasingly expensive to clear barrels from Northwest Europe.
The M1 and M2 FOB ARA Eurobob cracks moved closer over the week, with the M1 crack at a 99 cents/b premium to M2 on Oct. 22, narrowing by $3.56/b week over week, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Further along the curve, a contango structure continued to emerge. The M2/M3 FOB ARA crack spread stood at a 28 cents/b discount on Oct. 22, while the M3/M4 spread was at a $1.51/b discount, Platts data showed.
The convergence came amid weakness in European gasoline flat prices, with the backwardated prompt market continuing to incentivize holders to sell. At the same time, elevated freight costs have made it increasingly expensive to clear barrels from Northwest Europe, adding pressure to FOB export economics.
"Freight [is] becoming a barrier to doing stuff. It's expensive to go anywhere," a Northwest European gasoline trader said Oct. 23.
Platts assessed the cross-Channel UKC-UKC 22,000 mt clean tanker freight rate at $37.45/mt on Oct. 22, up 26.1% day over day and 78.2% month over month.
Trans-Atlantic freight has also risen, with the UKC-USAC 37,000 mt clean tanker rate at $31.31/mt on Oct. 22, up 30.7% month over month.

The trader said the trans-Atlantic arbitrage appeared more favorable further along the curve but remained closed in the prompt, with any reopening dependent on freight costs easing.
ARA was meanwhile seeing additional cargoes moving into the region, the trader said, while elevated freight costs limited the ability to clear barrels toward the Mediterranean or across the Atlantic.
Despite the weakness, the prompt gasoline market remained backwardated, providing an incentive to sell barrels as quickly as possible while demand remained available, the trader said. This could support further stock draws, although the trader expected cash differentials to weaken as the market moves further into winter.
Gasoline-naphtha spreads converge Gasoline-naphtha spreads have undergone a similar
convergence along the forward curve, with the prompt premium over later months narrowing sharply over the week.
The M1 gasoline-naphtha spread was $143.50/mt on Oct. 22, $3.25/mt above M2, with the premium narrowing by $25.25/mt week over week, Platts data showed. M1 was $1.50/mt above M3, with that premium narrowing by $33.50/mt over the same period.
The compression marks a significant retreat from the record highs previously seen in gasoline-naphtha spreads, reducing the incentive to pull naphtha into the gasoline blending pool.
"In the last week, we've seen some softness, but still volumes being pulled in the gasoline pool. Spread-wise, the incentive to put [naphtha] in gasoline has become a bit less just because of the economics," a trading source said.
With seasonal gasoline demand easing into winter, market participants expect the petrochemical sector to regain influence over European naphtha pricing.
"[In the] winter season [...] the pricing on naphtha is more driven by [the] petchem complex," the trading source said.
Rising propane prices have also reduced propane's competitiveness as a petrochemical feedstock, supporting an increase in petrochemical demand for naphtha over the past week and providing an additional source of support to naphtha relative to gasoline.

ARA gasoline stocks at 5-year low; naphtha stocks up on weak blending pull
High freight costs curb gasoline exports
Med tightness may spur ARA arbitrage in Nov
Naphtha stocks rise but stay below last year
Gasoline inventories in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub declined 14.41% to 707,000 metric tons in the week ended Oct. 22, marking a significant draw and hitting its lowest since Sept. 9, 2021, according to Insights Global data.
European gasoline prices have been declining amid a seasonal lull in demand and high freight rates across the region, limiting

barrel mobility and restricting export demand, sources said. "Freight is the biggest hurdle at the moment. Demurrage
is expensive as well, [and] delays are costly despite softer backwardation," an European trader said, adding that the broader market weakness reflected a combination of high freight costs and falling demand.
According to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, FOB AR Eurobob barges recovered from a two-month low on Oct. 22, rising $13.50/mt day over day but down 7.69% week over week to $1,098.75/mt.
Part of the destocking seen across Northwest European markets was incentivized by a severe backwardation structure over the week, prompting higher trading activity in the Platts Market on Close assessment process over the one-week period, with liquidity narrowing at the back end of the week.
As cash differentials have come off significantly and inventories have been drawn down, sources expect a slowdown in the rate of depreciation in market value.
The physical premium for FOB AR Eurobob barges over the equivalent November swap was at $54/mt on Oct. 22, narrowing $74/mt week over week.
While cash differentials were at a near four-week low, market participants raised concerns about narrowing margins amid the ongoing crude strength.
"Landed values [are] expensive for crude, [and] cracks cannot give up anymore from where they stand at around $19/b, unless crude softens," a Mediterranean analyst said.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB ARA Eurobob barge crack at $18.50/b on Oct. 22, down 15 cents/b day over day and $5.50/b week over week.
Meanwhile, a tightening supply picture in the Mediterranean sees some regional traders rallying expectations that renewed demand for ARA-Med arbitrages will emerge across the first half of November, though this depends on freight cooling, sources said.
"Med is getting tighter indeed, [and we] need to pull product from the ARA," a second European trader said. Eurobob had been selling off, making spreads more attractive, the trader added, "but [they] need to grow significantly more to make the arb happen."
A third European trader similarly said the Mediterranean was "running tight in product" and would "not cope without additional inflows from outside," although high freight costs remained an obstacle.
The November Med/North gasoline differential, the spread between the November FOB Med swaps and the equivalent FOB ARA Eurobob barge, was up $1.25/mt at a $21/mt premium, with the December spread 50 cents/mt wider at a $22/mt premium.
"Beginning of November [is] very tight," another trading source said, adding that there were "less and less avails" while "more and more people [are] asking for more barrels."

California gasoline stockpiles post second consecutive build
CARB gasoline stocks hit 4-month peak
Refiners boost non-CA fuel output 48% weekly
Total inventory climbs to 10.28M barrels
California's gasoline stockpiles rose during the week ended Oct. 16, building on their recent recovery and pushing above the 10-million-barrel mark as domestic fuel reserves swelled to multimonth highs, according to California Energy Commission data from Oct. 22.
Total gasoline inventories increased by 198,000 barrels week over week to 10.28 million barrels, marking the second consecutive week above the 10-million-barrel threshold. The steady accumulation over recent weeks has significantly improved the state's fuel cushion, lifting overall stockpiles above the 9.95 million barrels recorded two years ago, though reserves still trail the 11.38 million barrels held at this time last year.
The weekly build was driven almost entirely by in-state fuel supplies. Inventories of CARB reformulated gasoline -- the backbone of California's retail market -- surged by 284,000 barrels to 4.87 million barrels, the highest level since early August.
The domestic accumulation comfortably offset minor contractions across secondary categories, with blending component reserves down 32,000 barrels to 4.68 million and outof-state non-California grades down 54,000 barrels to 731,000 barrels.

On the refinery floor, overall operations held remarkably steady. Total inputs and production nudged up by 5,000 barrels to 5.37 million barrels, outpacing the 5.21 million barrels produced a year ago.
However, that calm topline masked a dramatic operational pivot beneath the surface. After weeks of ramping up domestic runs, refiners scaled back CARB reformulated gasoline production, trimming output by 215,000 barrels to 4.69 million barrels.
In its place, refiners aggressively ramped up their out-of-state lines. Non-California gasoline production leaped by 220,000 barrels -- a staggering 48% surge week over week -- to reach 675,000 barrels, its highest weekly volume since late August.
This operational shift suggests refiners seized opportunities to supply neighboring regional markets as domestic storage buffers strengthened. With CARB stockpiles well-supplied and refinery runs continuing to run ahead of year-ago levels, California's supply landscape enters late October on its most comfortable footing of the autumn season.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed San Francisco regular CARBOB at NYMEX December RBOB futures plus 40 cents/gallon and Los Angeles CARBOB at futures plus 38 cents/ gal Oct. 23.
-- Julia Martinez

The refinery hit the 650,000 b/d milestone for the first time in February, and has since operated at 97% of that capacity, according to NMDPRA data.
The company's diesel and gasoil production averaged 24.32 million l/d (153,000 b/d) in September, up from 18 million ld/ in August, while jet fuel production rose from 24.48 million l/d to 26.13 million l/d.

In tandem, the volumes of imported gasoline dropped from 14.6 million l/d in August to 10.2 million l/d in September -- a four month low. In contrast, domestic consumption recovered slightly, rising from 41.5 million l/d in September to 44.4 million l/d in August.
Nevertheless, demand remains well below levels before the outbreak of the US-Iran war, which has pushed up global prices and squeezed out price-sensitive consumption. Before the conflict erupted at the end of February, Nigeria was consuming some 56.9 million l/d of gasoline, according to NMDPRA figures.

For these international gasoline imports, the top two discharge ports are Suape (187,325 cubic meters) and Itaqui (123,789 cubic meters).
The Brazilian gasoline import arbitrage window has been closed for 240 days, according to an Oct. 23 report from the Brazilian Association of Fuels Importers, Abicom. Across Brazil's six primary transport fuel trading hubs, domestic prices average about 420 reais/cubic meter below import-parity prices.

NIGERIA DATA: Gasoline imports drop by 30% amid steady Dangote supply
Gasoline imports drop to four-month low in September
Dangote refinery operates at 106% capacity
Refinery increases production of main products
Nigeria's gasoline imports dropped by 30% month over month in September despite a shallow increase in local consumption, the country's downstream regulator reported, as the country's Dangote refinery continued to operate around full capacity.
In its monthly factsheet on the state of the downstream oil sector, the Nigerian Midstream Downstream Petroleum Regulatory Agency said that the Dangote refinery operated at

Import policy The drop in imports comes as the regulator has continued
to lock horns with Dangote over its import policy, with the refiner pushing for foreign deliveries to be excluded from the market. The NMDPRA on Sept. 18 issued 830,000 mt of gasoline import permits to six retail companies for Q4, in line with its recent policy.
The regulator is responsible for determining the appropriate import volume to ensure sufficient energy supplies for Nigeria, although Dangote has challenged the claim that it can fully meet all local demand.
According to its new report, the NMDPRA saw gasoline inventories remain roughly stable in September, with total stocks at 23.9 days. The previous month, the country had 22.9 days' worth of gasoline in storage, still below the regulator's 30day target.
"By 2027, the Authority aims to boost Nigeria's strategic (fuel) reserve to achieve 90 days of supply, strengthening national energy security and enhancing the resilience of the domestic petroleum supply system," a statement from NMDPRA quoted its Chief Executive, Rabiu Abdullahi Umar, as saying.
Nigeria's four state-owned refineries, with combined nameplate capacity to refine 445,000 b/d, remained offline during the period in review. However, four modular refineries, Waltermith, Edo, Aradel and OPAC continued to operate, contributing small volumes to the country's gasoil balance.

Japan's PM orders review of gasoline subsidy policy on concerns over fiscal burden
PM orders review of subsidy program
Current support reaches ¥42.80/liter
Fund balance stands at ¥560 billion
Japan's Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi announced Oct. 23 that she will order the Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry on the same day to examine the coming gasoline subsidy policy based on proposals from ruling as well as opposition parties, while keeping a close eye on an impact of the Middle Eastern situations on future inflation and economy, according to a website of the Prime Minister's Office of Japan.
"We will not change the policy next month, though we will be flexible on a support scheme including a subsidy amount and an exit strategy," Takaichi said. "We haven't decided yet."
Japan reinstated its gasoline subsidy policy in March to curb a jump in oil product prices coming from a surge in crude oil prices, according to METI. The current subsidy for oil products excluding jet fuel is ¥42.80/liter (27 cents/liter) for the week of Oct. 22-28.
Takaichi said she will instruct the Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry to coordinate with the Minister of Finance on securing the funds necessary for the policy.
The subsidy hit a record high at ¥51/l for Sept. 16-30, according to METI. The balance of the gasoline subsidy fund was around ¥560 billion at the end of September.
Takayuki Kobayashi, Chairperson of Policy Research Council in the ruling Liberal Democratic Party, said on Oct. 22 that the party will examine the subsidy policy as the government pays tens of billions of yen from taxes, according to a website of the LDP.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the average prices of gasoline, kerosene and gasoil at ¥99,525/kiloliter, ¥116,500/ kl and ¥100,800/kl, respectively, across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo and Hanshin regions Oct. 23.
-- Akihiro Gotoda
European reformate premiums hit multimonth low on gasoline strength
Reformate supply tightens as naphtha costs rise
Gasoline-naphtha spread drops to April low
Petrochemical demand for naphtha strengthens
European reformate premiums fell to multimonth lows Oct. 22 as recovering Eurobob gasoline prices outpaced reformate, while firm naphtha values continued to squeeze reformer economics and complicate the supply outlook.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed FOB AR reformate barges up $13.50/mt at $1,214.50/mt on Oct. 22, rebounding from an Aug. 11 low. Despite the daily recovery, reformate prices remained $109.50/mt lower week over week.
The reformate premium over the front-month E5 Eurobob swap fell $3.50/mt to $169.75/mt on Oct. 22, its lowest since July 31, amid renewed strength in the underlying gasoline market.

Market participants said reformate remained difficult to source, with firm naphtha prices squeezing reformer economics and encouraging refiners to retain higher-value blending components.
"Naphtha demand [is] good in the East, pulling, making reformate production margins slimmer," a Mediterranean gasoline trader said. "Blending demand [has been] coming in [over] the last two to three weeks, and the naphtha strength [is] making it tricky."
A European gasoline trader similarly described reformate as "challenging to source," adding that refiners had little incentive to sell components into the merchant market.
"[It's] difficult fundamentally to find any of these components at the moment. The refineries keep them internally," the second trader said.
The gasoline-naphtha spread has meanwhile fallen to a multimonth low, weakening the relative economics of gasoline against its naphtha feedstock.
Platts assessed the front-month gasoline-naphtha spread at $143/mt on Oct. 22, down $4.50/mt day over day, its lowest since April 9.
Reformate pricing has also faced competing regional signals. According to the Med trader, the US had been paying better for reformate over the previous six weeks, while European values had been falling.
Despite the constrained supply picture, some signs of easing have emerged for early November. The European trader said reformate and component availability remained difficult, but were starting to improve.
"[I'm] starting to see a little softness, pricing coming off, but it's still tricky," he said. "For early November, [it's] safe to say premiums are falling" as some material was becoming available inland in railcars and storage.
Meanwhile, naphtha inventories in the ARA hub rose 11.4% in the week ended Oct. 22 to 363,000 mt, Insights Global data showed.
With seasonal gasoline demand easing into winter, market participants expect the petrochemical sector to regain influence over naphtha pricing.
"[In the] winter season ... the pricing on naphtha is more driven by [the] petchem complex," another naphtha trading source said.
Rising propane prices have also eroded its competitiveness as a petrochemical feedstock, prompting an uptick in petrochemical demand for naphtha over the past week.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha swap up $21.50/mt at $901.75/mt on Oct. 22, while the equivalent crack strengthened 23 cents/b to minus $5.60/b.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Friedrich Gutsche

Platts European Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
ARA gasoline inventories drop 14% to lowest since 2021
High freight costs limit export opportunities
Prompt crack spreads compress amid destocking
European gasoline markets strengthened Oct. 23, supported by falling regional inventories, while elevated freight costs continued to restrict the movement of barrels and weigh on export economics.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed FOB AR Eurobob gasoline barges up $30.75/metric ton at $1,129.50/mt on Oct. 23, while the equivalent cash differential narrowed 50 cents/mt to $53.50/mt.
Gasoline inventories in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub fell 14.41% to 707,000 metric tons in the week ended Oct. 22, their lowest level since Sept. 9, 2021, according to Insights Global data.
The stock draw came against a backdrop of softer seasonal gasoline demand and high freight costs, which have constrained barrel mobility and limited export opportunities, market sources said.
"Freight is the biggest hurdle at the moment. Demurrage is expensive as well, [and] delays are costly despite softer backwardation," a European trader said, adding that broader market weakness reflected a combination of high freight costs and declining demand.
A steeply backwardated prompt structure had encouraged destocking across Northwest Europe during the week, while trading activity in the Platts Market on Close assessment process was stronger earlier in the period before liquidity thinned toward the end of the week.

At the same time, European gasoline cracks compressed sharply across the prompt forward curve in the week, according to Platts analysis on Oct. 23. The M1 FOB ARA Eurobob crack was at a 71 cents/b premium to M2 on Oct. 23, with the spread narrowing by $3.29/b week over week, according to Platts data.
Further forward, the curve moved into contango. The M2/M3 FOB ARA crack spread was at a 61 cents/b discount on Oct. 23, while M3/M4 stood at a $2.06/b discount.
The flattening of the prompt crack structure coincided with weaker European gasoline flat prices, with the remaining backwardation continuing to encourage holders to release prompt barrels. Meanwhile, high freight costs made clearing surplus material from Northwest Europe increasingly expensive, adding pressure to FOB export economics.
"Freight [is] becoming a barrier to doing stuff. It's expensive to go anywhere," a Northwest European gasoline trader said Oct. 23.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB AR Eurobob barge at $1,076/mt, up $31.25/mt on the day, with the equivalent crack down 63 cents/b at $17.87/b.
The November/December spread rose $1.75/mt to $42/mt, while December/January also gained $1.75/mt to $23.75/mt.
The November Mediterranean/North gasoline differential, representing the spread between the November FOB Mediterranean swap and the equivalent FOB ARA Eurobob barge, was unchanged at a $21/mt premium. The December differential was also unchanged, at a $22/mt premium.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Friedrich Gutsche

October supply overhang pressures market
Record-high freight rates thin buying interest further
Bearish market fundamentals, driven by a supply overhang from October cargoes and lackluster demand ahead of winter, pushed the spot cash differential for Asian jet fuel/kerosene cargoes into discount territory on Oct. 23.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene cargo cash differential at minus 18 cents/barrel to the Mean of Platts Singapore jet fuel/kerosene assessments at the Oct. 23 Asian close, slipping 30 cents/b day over day from plus 12 cents/b the previous session.
During the Platts Singapore physical jet fuel/kerosene Market on Close process on Oct. 23, Unipec bought a Nov. 7-11 loading cargo from Vitol at MOPS minus 48 cents/b after normalization.
The cash differential was last lower at minus $1.28/b on Sept. 23, according to Platts data, marking its weakest value in a month.
The shift into negative territory coincided with a fresh wave of offers for November-loading spot cargoes from South Korea, adding to a glut that has weighed on the market since China resumed refined product exports following a brief suspension during the Golden Week holiday.
Meanwhile, record-high freight rates have further weighed on buying interest, compounding already subdued demand amid limited regional shorts, sources said.
"The overhang from October is huge, plus freight is killing everything," said a Singapore-based jet fuel trader.
"Buying interest is very thin at the moment, we don't see any big buy tenders for jet, and freight is very expensive. Moving cargoes is still very expensive despite lower FOB spot prices," said a second participant based in Singapore, adding that arbitrage

opportunities have also been dampened. South Korean spot cargo differentials have also come under
pressure, tumbling to steep discounts amid bearish market sentiment.
S-Oil sold 300,000 barrels of jet A-1 fuel for loading Nov. 22-26 at a discount of about 80 cents-$1/b to the November wholemonth average of the Mean of Platts Singapore jet fuel/kerosene assessments, FOB Onsan.
Another South Korean refiner was heard to have sold two MR cargoes of jet A-1 fuel for loading Nov. 20-22 and Nov. 28-30 at respective discounts of about $1.50/b and $1.80-$2/b to the November whole-month average of the MOPS jet fuel/kerosene assessments, FOB.
In comparison, earlier traded first-half November loading cargoes concluded at premiums of $2-$3.50/b, trade sources said.
GS Caltex canceled its tender offering 300,000 or 450,000 barrels of jet A-1 fuel for loading over Nov. 8-12 from Yeosu. The tender was set to close Oct. 23, with same-day validity.
The exact reason for the cancellation could not be confirmed, but some market participants attributed it to potentially low bids.
"With current freight levels, it's difficult to give good bids," said a third jet fuel trader.
Platts assessed the lump sum 30,000-mt MR clean tanker freight rate on the South Korea-Singapore route at $2.2 million on Oct. 23, the highest since the assessment started in October 1998.

Russian diesel tanker heads to New York as Kremlin claims regular shipments
Malta-flagged tanker should carry 40,000 mt fuel
Agioi Fanendes left Primorsk Oct. 22: CAS
US lifts sanctions, enables Russian imports
The Malta-flagged medium-sized tanker Agioi Fanendes is heading to New York carrying Russian diesel, according to S&P Global Energy Commodities at Sea data, with the Kremlin expecting deliveries to continue "on a regular basis."
The Kremlin's spokesperson, Dmitry Peskov, said Oct. 23 that the first tanker carrying Russian diesel was sent to the US.
"The first tanker has, in fact, set off toward its destinations -- to its buyers -- and [subsequent shipments] will continue on a regular basis," Peskov told journalists, according to TASS. He did not clarify the name of the vessel sent to the US or who the buyers of the delivery are.
The Agioi Fanendes tanker left Russia's port of Primorsk on Oct. 22 and is expected to reach New York around Nov. 6-7, according to CAS data. It is unclear who the buyer and the seller of the vessel might be.
The first diesel tanker from Russia to the US since 2022 is expected to carry about 40,000 metric tons, Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said Oct. 21.
Eastern Mediterranean Maritime, which manages the Agioi Fanendes, according to its website, could not be reached for comment Oct. 23.

While the vessel remains in the Baltic Sea, it can still divert its destination.
Market reaction The delivery of these diesel volumes to the US "would only be
a displacement of volume from Brazil," according to a European diesel trader who asked for anonymity as not authorized to speak publicly. "It is a logistical change regarding where the product goes, rather than an increase in flows. We would have to see vastly more flows for it to [affect] the European market."
Shipping market participants were also seeing signs of additional Russian-loading opportunities. A Greece-based broker said UAN and clean petroleum product stems were emerging, with New York, Houston and Canada's Come By Chance among potential destinations, although fixtures had yet to be confirmed.
The emergence of further Russian-loading stems could also have implications for the European MR tanker market. Shipping sources said additional Russian business could draw vessels away from conventional European employment, while a larger pool of owners willing to lift Russian cargoes could put downward pressure on freight rates for Russian-loading voyages.
Russia was the second-largest diesel exporter globally before a major fuel crisis began in the country this summer, triggered by Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries. Turkey, Brazil and Egypt were the main buyers of Russian flows in the past years after the EU banned Russian fuel products in response to the country's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.
The US Treasury Department's Office of Foreign Assets Control granted on Oct. 9 a sanctions waiver to Russia allowing the sale, delivery and import of Russian-origin diesel fuel, including into the US. In response, the Russian government has partially lifted its full diesel export ban introduced in July.
Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has been under pressure from US President Donald Trump to stop attacks on Russian refineries. The last time Ukraine struck a Russian refinery was on Oct. 15, marking a slowdown in Ukraine's campaign targeting Russian energy infrastructure.
It remains unclear how much additional diesel Russia can export to global markets in the coming months.
Novak said Oct. 9 that Russia is ready to export 300,000 mt of diesel in October, 500,000 mt in November and 1 million mt in December. He later said that Russian diesel deliveries can reach up to 3.5 million mt/month by the end of 2026, without clarifying the discrepancy between the figures.
Zelenskyy said Oct. 15 that US military officials told their counterparts that if Ukraine did not stop the energy strikes, the US would block intelligence sharing with Ukraine. But Ukraine is only ready to stop its attacks on Russian refineries if Russia stops striking its energy infrastructure and killing Ukrainians, Zelenskyy said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the European diesel benchmark ULSD 10 ppm CIF NWE Cargo price at $1,479 / mt on Oct. 22, up from $1,470.5/mt on Oct. 22, but still well below

ARA diesel, gasoil stocks inch down week over week: Insights Global
ARA diesel/gasoil stocks fall 0.45% to 1.774 mil mt
European tightness amid closed trans-Atlantic arbitrage
Diesel and gasoil stocks in the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp refining hub declined 0.45% to 1.774 million metric tons in the week ended Oct. 22, the latest data from Insights Global showed. Despite the modest weekly decline, inventories remain substantially depleted year over year, standing 17.95% below the same week in 2025, when stocks were at 2.162 million mt.
The physical market showed strength during the week, with prices advancing across both diesel and gasoil. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 10 ppm diesel FOB ARA barge flat price up $15.25/mt at $1,454.50/mt Oct. 22, above the $1,432.25/mt average for October so far. The premium-grade fuel continues to trade at elevated levels as supply constraints persist across the European market.
The European supply crunch has been exacerbated by weak trans-Atlantic arbitrage incentives and the local prioritization of jet fuel production amid a negative diesel-jet regrade in Europe.
The US Gulf Coast-Europe diesel arbitrage stood closed at minus 11 cents/b on Oct. 21, according to Platts data, discouraging flows across the Atlantic. Meanwhile, the physical AmsterdamRotterdam-Antwerp diesel crack was at $73.66/b Oct. 21, implying a $13.68/b regrade in favor of jet fuel output.
Similarly, the 50 ppm gasoil barge price gained $15.25/mt to reach $1,424.25/mt Oct. 22, marking a 1.08% increase on the day. The 50 ppm gasoil specification, used as heating fuel in Switzerland and Germany, has maintained firm pricing as the heating season approaches.
Sources have said that Europe's heating oil demand season is beginning to pick up heading into winter; however, buyers seem to be holding back on seasonal buying appetite as gasoil flat prices have roughly doubled from a year earlier.
For comparison, the flat price of 50 ppm gasoil barges stood at $745.75/mt Feb. 27, the day before the Middle East war started.
-- Sophia Aung, David Neef
ARA fuel oil stocks fall 0.85% week over week: Insights Global
VLSFO arbitrage open to Americas and Asia
Rotterdam terminal faces six-day loading delays
Fuel oil stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub fell 0.85% to 930,000 metric tons in the week to

Oct. 22, Insights Global data showed, with stocks building in the previous week.
Fuel oil's share of overall oil product inventories in the ARA region rose to 22%.
In the Northwest European VLSFO market, higher flat prices weighed on end-user buying appetite. However, elevated premiums in the Americas and Asian markets have led to volume flowing out of Europe on the arbitrage, thereby supporting prices.
Market participants noted ample availability of blendstocks such as low-sulfur straight run, where a well-supplied market has pushed blenders to sell straight-run product into the VLSFO bunkering pool.
The front-month EU fuel oil 0.5%S marine fuel oil structure was assessed at a backwardation of $14.50/mt on Oct. 22, narrowing $1.25/mt week over week.
In the high sulfur fuel oil market, participants describe fundamentals as "balanced." However, there are some resupply concerns centered around the closed arbitrage window from the Americas region.
"Arbitrage from Americas is dynamic, differentials in Europe are high, but so is freight, not that many cargoes are coming to Europe," a Europe-based trader source said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 3.5%S fuel oil FOB Rotterdam barge front-month time spread equivalent at a backwardation of $24.25/mt on Oct. 22, widening $2.25/mt week over week.
Platts assessed the front-month HSFO crack spread at minus $8.682/barrel, down 75.3 cents/b from minus $7.929/b the previous week.
The Northwest European bunker market remained subdued this week, with weak demand in the ARA hub set against tighter prompt availability for some grades.
In Rotterdam, HSFO loading delays limited flexibility for prompt stems at a terminal.
Market participants reported waiting more than six days for HSFO at the loading rack, prompting one trader to seek alternatives.
Despite these pockets of tightness, availability across Rotterdam was broadly adequate.
Participants pointed to congestion and product not being ready, though the cause of the disruption remained unclear.
Prompt supply was tighter in Amsterdam, where a few suppliers could cover HSFO stems within the week, and HSFO and VLSFO were at times unavailable for delivery in the week ending Oct. 24.
MGO was also tight in Amsterdam, though slightly better supplied than fuel oil.
Demand remained slow to poor, as the RED III cost disadvantage in the Netherlands compared with nearby ports continued to weigh on buying activity.
Rising flat prices also kept buyers on the sidelines while they waited for a clear price direction.
-- Tommy Petrou, Alexander Wong, Iman Rezig

SINGAPORE DATA: Middle distillate stocks fall 20% to 8.31 million barrels
Gasoil exports jump 80.2% on week to 367,039 mt
Jet fuel imports double, exports halve on week
Singapore's onshore commercial middle distillate stocks plunged 19.97% week over week from a near six-month high of 10.38 million barrels to 8.31 million barrels over Oct. 15-21, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 22.
The city-state reverted to a net exporter of gasoil in the week ended Oct. 21, as outflows surged 80.17% to 367,039 metric tons.
Indonesia and Australia were the largest destinations with 89,136 mt and 78,492 mt, respectively.
This was followed by Malaysia (37,268 mt), Togo (29,582 mt) and Cameroon (29,984 mt).
New Zealand and Reunion also absorbed 25,706 mt and 21,684 mt, respectively.
The remaining exports flowed to six other countries, led by Myanmar with 15,508 mt, Vietnam with 14,280 mt, and the Philippines with 12,691 mt.
Meanwhile, imports retreated 13.59% week over week to 220,955 mt over Oct. 15-21, Enterprise Singapore data showed.
Shipments from China fell 19.98% week over week to 82,035 mt, while volumes from Malaysia had also decreased 38.54% week over week to 39,824 mt.
Imports from South Korea, however, had surged 78.68% week over week to 99,095 mt, placing it as the city-state's top supplier.
Jet fuel exports halve Singapore remained a net exporter of jet fuel/kerosene over
Oct. 15-21 even as imports doubled, while outflows halved. Jet fuel imports jumped to 11,955 mt from 6,176 mt the week
before, with China the dominant supplier. In contrast, exports fell 45.9% week over week to 50,278 mt. The downswing came as outflows to the Netherlands fell
to zero from 38,485 mt the previous week, while shipments to the top destination, Australia, fell 38.33% to 24,867 mt over the period.
The remaining exports were absorbed by Reunion with 12,054 mt, Indonesia with 7,583 mt and New Zealand with 5,767 mt.
-- Shu ling Lee, Mei huey Ng
India diesel exports to Europe hit record high in Oct on lack of US inflows
India ships record 1.3 mil mt diesel to Europe
US-Europe arbitrage closes
About 1.3 million metric tons of diesel/gasoil is expected to arrive in Europe from India in October, the highest volume on record for this route, according to Kpler and far higher than the 573,000 mt shipped in September.
Over Jan. 1-Sept. 30, India exported a total of 2.5 million mt to

Europe, according to Kpler. During the first few months of the conflict in the Middle East,
monthly Indian diesel/gasoil exports to Europe were subdued as strong arbitrage incentives from Singapore drew barrels eastwards to supply the Asian market.
The recent rise in Indian exports to Europe comes against the backdrop of severe physical tightness in Europe, exacerbated by weak trans-Atlantic arbitrage incentives and the local prioritization of jet fuel production amid a negative diesel-jet regrade in Europe.
The US Gulf Coast-Europe diesel arbitrage stood closed at minus 11 cents/b on Oct. 21, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, discouraging flows across the Atlantic. Meanwhile, the physical Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp diesel crack was $73.66/b on Oct. 21, implying a $13.68/b regrade in favor of jet fuel output.
"Europe is still tight and we see the USGC arb[itrage] shut, so barrels from there are likely going to Latin America. So Europe... seems to be pulling from India and what it can get out of AG," said an Europe-based diesel source.
US diesel and gasoil exports to Europe are expected to reach around 1.2 million mt during the month of October, lower compared to the 1.8 million mt total in September and 1.4 million mt that arrived in October 2025, according to Kpler
However, the same source cautioned that "Asia should tighten as China reduces exports," which could reduce the availability of products for export to Europe.
"Asia is a crude supply story as much as anything else," the same source said.
Disruptions to shipping through the Bab al-Mandeb Strait stand to jeopardize both crude inputs to and westward refinedproduct exports from Asian refineries.
At Bab al-Mandab, activity moved sharply lower, with total transit falling to 34 from 44 on Oct. 20, according to a latest report from S&P Global MINT and S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Despite the current uptick in Indian diesel exports to Europe, market participants are carefully monitoring factors that could threaten the supply route and plunge Europe into further tightness.
-- Sophia Aung, David Neef
Pakistan's July-Sept fuel oil exports drop amid power generation feed mix shift
HSFO exports down 12% YOY, LSFO down 3%
Fuel oil fills in for lower LNG imports
Generation from fuel oil nearly triples
Pakistan's fuel oil exports fell 12% year over year in the JulySeptember quarter, as reduced liquefied natural gas imports amid the Middle East conflict boosted demand for furnace oil in power generation, prompting refineries to divert supplies to the domestic market.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 23, 2026

High sulfur fuel oil exports declined to 246,417 mt in the first quarter of the fiscal year 2026-2027, from 279,882 mt in the corresponding period a year earlier, data from the Oil Companies Advisory Council, which compiles petroleum product consumption, import and export figures, showed Oct. 22.
Exports of low sulfur fuel oil fell 3% to 52,597 mt from 54,302 mt over the same period, the data showed.
Mohammad Awais Ashraf, director of research at AKD Securities, said that lower LNG imports forced the government to increase reliance on fuel oil and coal for electricity generation.
Pakistan received just eight LNG cargoes during the quarter, down from 28 cargoes in the corresponding period last year, Ashraf said.
Electricity generation from fuel oil-fired power plants nearly tripled to 799 gigawatt-hours in the July-September quarter, from 297 GWh a year earlier, data from the National Electric Power Regulatory Authority showed.
Generation from regasified LNG-fired plants, meanwhile, fell to 3,744 GWh from 6,744 GWh over the same period, the data showed.
Pakistan has had to operate fuel oil-fired power plants to help reduce electricity load-shedding hours following a sharp decline in RLNG plant power generation.
The shift in demand has prompted even coastal refineries, which have traditionally exported furnace oil, to consider supplying part of their output to the domestic market, said Asad Ali, research analyst at Topline Securities, on Oct. 23.
Refineries had been exporting furnace oil for an extended period because of the petroleum levy imposed by the government, Ali said. However, the recent shortage of RLNG has increased demand for furnace oil in the power sector, he added.
Structural pressure on fuel oil exports Despite the near-term increase in domestic demand, furnace
oil exports could rise as local consumption remains structurally weak, said Muhammad Bilal Ejaz, research analyst at Ismail Iqbal Securities, on Oct. 23.
Petroleum and carbon levies, alongside the shift in power generation toward gas, coal and solar energy, have left refineries with surplus fuel oil volumes that must be placed in overseas markets, Ejaz said.
Pakistan exported 1.435 million mt of HSFO in fiscal year 202526, up from 1.3 million mt in the previous fiscal year, according to OCAC data.
Exports of LSFO rose to 180,469 mt from 137,880 mt over the same period, the data showed.
The Oil and Gas Regulatory Authority has allowed four refineries to export about 195,000 mt of fuel oil in October to help reduce accumulated stocks at terminals, according to an OGRA notification dated Sept. 30 reviewed by Platts on Oct. 14 through an industry source.
The refineries covered by the authorization are Pak-Arab Refinery Ltd., National Refinery Ltd., Pakistan Refinery Ltd. and Cnergyico, the notification showed.

Asian HSFO markets strengthen Asian HSFO markets are drawing support from expectations of
reduced November and December arrivals, as record-high freight costs weigh on arbitrage flows, while escalating ship attacks heightened the risk of lifting supply from the Persian Gulf.
Platts assessed the Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo cash differential over Mean of Platts Singapore 380 CST HSFO at a premium of $48.65/mt at the Asian close Oct. 23, up $2.73/mt day over day.
The Singapore 180 CST HSFO cargo cash differential against the Mean of Platts Singapore 180 CST HSFO was assessed at a premium of $45.92/mt, up $2.13/mt from the preceding session.
The strong distillates complex and wide gasoil-fuel oil spread limited heavy gasoil components from entering the HSFO blend pool as viscosity cutters, according to a trader.
-- Iris Leung, Haris Zamir
Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Gasoil Daily Market Analysis
Thailand's SPRC offers 33,000 mt of gasoil for Nov. 7-9 loading
Singapore's gasoil exports jump 80.2% on week to 367,039 mt
The Asian ultralow sulfur gasoil market saw backwardation widen on Oct. 23, amid heightened geopolitical tensions.
Brokers pegged the front-month November-December Singapore gasoil swaps time spread at $3.40/b in midafternoon Asian trading Oct. 23, widening from $3/b at the 0830 GMT Asian close Oct. 22.
Moscow launched another missile attack on the Ukrainian capital Kyiv, clouding prospects for a de-escalation just hours after Russian President Vladimir Putin signaled readiness to resume trilateral peace talks with Ukraine and the US.
Russia will work with the US on more energy deals, Kirill Dmitriev, Russian special presidential envoy for investment and economic cooperation with foreign countries, said Oct. 22.
In physical cargoes, the benchmark Platts-assessed cash differential for FOB Singapore gasoil with a maximum sulfur content of 10 parts per million dipped 9 cents/b lower day over day to 95 cents/b at the 0830 GMT Asian close Oct. 22.
Spot market activity had been limited relative to co-distillate jet fuel/kerosene, as refiners favor the production of the latter to fulfill winter demand, according to Asia-based market participants.
The front-month November regrade swap -- the value jet fuel commands over 10 parts per million sulfur gasoil -- averaged plus $1.65/barrel over Oct. 1-22, flipping from the positives from an average of minus $3.44/b over September, Platts data showed.
On tenders, Thailand's SPRC had offered 33,000 mt of 50 ppm sulfur gasoil loading over Nov. 7-9 from Map Ta Phut via a tender that closed on Oct. 22.
SPRC had also previously issued a tender offering a 33,000 mt cargo loading over Nov. 3-5 and a 15,000 mt cargo loading over Nov. 6-7 of 50 ppm sulfur gasoil that closed on Oct. 20, though the results of the tender could not yet be ascertained.
